1#1768Merge Strings AlternatelyEasy

You are given two strings, word1 and word2. Build a new string by taking letters alternately: first letter of word1, first letter of word2, second letter of word1, second of word2, and so on. If one string runs out, append all the remaining letters of the other string. Return the merged string. Example: 'abc' and 'pq' give a, p, b, q, then the leftover c → 'apbqc'.

Example input"abc", "pq"
→
Output"apbqc"

Why? 'abc' and 'pq': take a, p, b, q. Only 'c' is left, so add it → apbqc.

💡 Key idea Use one index i. Take word1[i], then word2[i], if each still exists.

Edge / failure case '','xyz' → 'xyz'
word1 is empty, so nothing alternates — the answer is just word2.

Open walkthrough ▶
2#1071Greatest Common Divisor of StringsEasy

A string t 'divides' a string s if s is t repeated one or more times (t + t + … = s). Given two strings str1 and str2, return the longest string x that divides BOTH of them, or an empty string if there is none. Example: 'ABCABCABC' is 'ABC' three times and 'ABCABC' is 'ABC' twice, so the answer is 'ABC'.

Example input"ABCABCABC", "ABCABC"
→
Output"ABC"

Why? 'ABCABCABC' is ABC three times. 'ABCABC' is ABC two times. So ABC divides both.

💡 Key idea If str1+str2 ≠ str2+str1 there is no answer. Otherwise the answer's length is gcd(len1, len2).

Edge / failure case 'LEET','CODE' → ''
No string repeats to build both words, so the answer is the empty string.

Open walkthrough ▶
3#1431Kids With the Greatest Number of CandiesEasy

candies[i] is how many candies kid i has, and you also have extraCandies to give. For each kid, imagine giving ALL the extra candies to just that kid. Return a list of true/false, one per kid: true if that kid would then have at least as many candies as the kid with the most candies (ties count as true). Example: candies=[2,3,5,1,3], extra=3 → the biggest pile is 5; kid with 1 candy gets 4 < 5, so false; everyone else reaches 5 or more.

Example inputcandies=[2,3,5,1,3], extra=3
→
Output[T, T, T, F, T]

Why? Most candies is 5, extra is 3. Kid with 2 → 5 ✓, 3 → 6 ✓, 5 → 8 ✓, 1 → 4 ✗, 3 → 6 ✓.

💡 Key idea Find the maximum once. Then check candies[i] + extra ≥ max.

Edge / failure case [4,2,1,1,2],1 → [True, False, False, False, False]
Only the kid who already has the most candies keeps up after +1.

Open walkthrough ▶
4#605Can Place FlowersEasy

flowerbed is a list where 0 means an empty plot and 1 means a plot with a flower. Flowers can't be planted in adjacent plots (two 1s may never sit side by side). Given an integer n, return true if you can plant n NEW flowers without breaking that rule, otherwise false. Existing flowers never move. Example: [1,0,0,0,1] with n=1 → only the middle plot is free of neighbours, so true.

Example inputflowerbed=[1,0,0,0,1], n=1
→
OutputTrue

Why? [1,0,0,0,1]: only the middle plot has empty plots on both sides. Plant there → one flower fits → True.

💡 Key idea Go left to right. Plant whenever a plot and both its neighbours are empty.

Edge / failure case [1,0,0,0,1],2 → False
Only the middle plot is free, so just 1 flower fits — we need 2.

Open walkthrough ▶
5#345Reverse Vowels of a StringEasy

Given a string s, reverse only its vowels (a, e, i, o, u — both lower and upper case) and return the result. All other characters stay exactly where they are. Example: 'IceCreAm' has vowels I, e, e, A (in that order); reversed they become A, e, e, I put back into the same vowel positions → 'AceCreIm'.

Example input"IceCreAm"
→
Output"AceCreIm"

Why? IceCreAm: the vowels in order are I, e, e, A. Reversed they are A, e, e, I. Put them back → AceCreIm.

💡 Key idea Two pointers from both ends. Skip non-vowels. Swap vowels.

Edge / failure case 'bcd' → 'bcd'
There are no vowels, so nothing changes.

Open walkthrough ▶
6#151Reverse Words in a StringMedium

Given a string s that contains words separated by spaces (there may be extra spaces at the start, at the end, or several between words), return the words in REVERSE order, joined by exactly one space, with no leading or trailing spaces. Example: ' the sky is blue ' has words the, sky, is, blue → 'blue is sky the'.

Example input" the sky is blue "
→
Output"blue is sky the"

Why? ' the sky is blue ' has the words the, sky, is, blue. Reversed → blue is sky the.

💡 Key idea split() already removes extra spaces. Reverse the word list and join with a space.

Edge / failure case 'a good example' → 'example good a'
Extra spaces between words collapse to a single space.

Open walkthrough ▶
7#238Product of Array Except SelfMedium

Given a list nums, return a list answer of the same length where answer[i] is the product of ALL numbers in nums except nums[i]. You may not use division. Aim for O(n) (time grows only in step with the input size) time. Example: [1,2,3,4] → answer[0]=2·3·4=24, answer[1]=1·3·4=12, answer[2]=1·2·4=8, answer[3]=1·2·3=6.

Example input[1, 2, 3, 4]
→
Output[24, 12, 8, 6]

Why? [1,2,3,4]: position 0 → 2×3×4 = 24. Position 1 → 1×3×4 = 12. Position 2 → 1×2×4 = 8. Position 3 → 1×2×3 = 6.

💡 Key idea answer[i] = (product of everything left of i) × (product of everything right of i).

Edge / failure case [-1,1,0,-3,3] → [0, 0, 9, 0, 0]
A zero makes every product 0 except the one at the zero's own position.

Open walkthrough ▶
8#334Increasing Triplet SubsequenceMedium

Given a list nums, return true if there are three positions i < j < k such that nums[i] < nums[j] < nums[k] — three numbers in left-to-right order that strictly increase (they don't have to be next to each other). Otherwise return false. Example: [2,1,5,0,4,6] contains 2 < 5 < 6 (or 1 < 4 < 6), so true.

Example input[2, 1, 5, 0, 4, 6]
→
OutputTrue

Why? [2,1,5,0,4,6]: 0, then 4, then 6 keep growing in order → True.

💡 Key idea Track the smallest value and the smallest 'middle' value so far. A number bigger than both proves a triplet.

Edge / failure case [5,4,3,2,1] → False
The numbers only go down, so no increasing triplet exists.

Open walkthrough ▶
9#443String CompressionMedium

chars is a list of single characters. Compress it IN PLACE: for each run of the same character, write the character, and if the run is longer than 1, also write the run length as separate digit characters (12 → '1','2'). Return the new length of the list; only the first that-many entries matter. Example: a,a,b,b,c,c,c → a,2,b,2,c,3 (length 6).

Example input["a","a","b","b","c","c","c"]
→
Output6 → ["a","2","b","2","c","3"]

Why? a a b b c c c becomes a 2 b 2 c 3. That is 6 characters long.

💡 Key idea One pointer reads ahead to count a run. Another writes the result behind it.

Edge / failure case ['a'] → [1, ['a']]
A single letter has a run of 1, so no count is written.

Open walkthrough ▶
10#283Move ZeroesEasy

Given a list nums, move all the 0s to the end while keeping the relative order of the non-zero numbers. Do it IN PLACE (change the same list, don't create a new one) and return nothing. Example: [0,1,0,3,12] becomes [1,3,12,0,0].

Example input[0, 1, 0, 3, 12]
→
Output[1, 3, 12, 0, 0]

Why? [0,1,0,3,12]: the non-zeros 1, 3, 12 slide left and the zeros go to the end → [1,3,12,0,0].

💡 Key idea Slow pointer = next spot for a non-zero. Swap each non-zero into that spot.

Edge / failure case [0] → [0]
A lone zero has nowhere to move — the list stays the same.

Open walkthrough ▶
11#392Is SubsequenceEasy

Given two strings s and t, return true if s is a subsequence of t — meaning you can get s by deleting zero or more letters from t WITHOUT changing the order of the remaining letters. Example: s='ace', t='abcde' → delete b and d and you are left with 'ace', so true. s='aec' would be false because e comes after c in t.

Example inputs="ace", t="abcde"
→
OutputTrue

Why? t = abcde. Delete b and d and you get ace → True.

💡 Key idea Scan t once. Move forward in s each time the letters match.

Edge / failure case 'axc','ahbgdc' → False
'x' never appears in 'ahbgdc', so 'axc' cannot be formed in order → False.

Open walkthrough ▶
12#11Container With Most WaterMedium

height[i] is the height of a vertical line standing at position i on the x-axis (line i goes from (i,0) up to (i,height[i])). Choose any two lines; together with the x-axis they form a container. Water level is limited by the SHORTER of the two lines, and the container's width is the distance between their positions, so the water held = min(height[i], height[j]) × (j − i). Return the largest amount of water any pair can hold. The container can't be tilted. Example: lines at positions 1 and 8 (heights 8 and 7) hold min(8,7) × 7 = 49.

Example input[1, 8, 6, 2, 5, 4, 8, 3, 7]
→
Output49

Why? Walls of height 8 (position 1) and 7 (position 8): width is 7, height is the shorter wall 7 → 7×7 = 49.

💡 Key idea Start with the widest pair. Always move the shorter wall inward, because moving the taller one can't help.

Edge / failure case [1,1] → 1
Only two walls of height 1, one unit apart → area 1.

Open walkthrough ▶
13#1679Max Number of K-Sum PairsMedium

Given a list nums and an integer k, you may repeat this operation: pick two numbers in the list that add up to k and remove both. Return the maximum number of operations you can perform. Example: nums=[3,1,3,4,3], k=6 → remove one pair (3,3); the remaining 1, 4, 3 contain no other pair summing to 6, so the answer is 1.

Example input[3, 1, 3, 4, 3], k=6
→
Output1

Why? [3,1,3,4,3], k=6: only the two 3s add up to 6. Nothing else pairs → 1 operation.

💡 Key idea Sort first. Then use one pointer at each end.

Edge / failure case [1,2,3,4],10 → 0
No two numbers add up to 10, so no operation is possible.

Open walkthrough ▶
14#643Maximum Average Subarray IEasy

Given a list nums and an integer k, look at every subarray (a run of consecutive numbers) of length exactly k. Return the largest average among them. Example: nums=[1,12,-5,-6,50,3], k=4 → the window 12,-5,-6,50 has sum 51, average 12.75, which is the best.

Example input[1, 12, -5, -6, 50, 3], k=4
→
Output12.75

Why? k=4: the run [12, -5, -6, 50] adds to 51, the biggest sum. 51 ÷ 4 = 12.75.

💡 Key idea Slide a window one step at a time: add the new number, subtract the one that left.

Edge / failure case [5],1 → 5
A single element is its own window.

Open walkthrough ▶
15#1456Maximum Vowels in a Substring of Given LengthMedium

Given a string s and an integer k, look at every substring (consecutive letters) of length exactly k. Return the largest number of vowels (a, e, i, o, u) found in any such substring. Example: s='abciiidef', k=3 → 'iii' contains 3 vowels, the maximum possible.

Example input"abciiidef", k=3
→
Output3

Why? k=3: the stretch 'iii' has 3 vowels, which is the most.

💡 Key idea Slide a window. Add 1 if the new letter is a vowel, subtract 1 if the leaving letter was.

Edge / failure case 'rhythm',3 → 0
'rhythm' contains no a, e, i, o or u, so every window has 0 vowels.

Open walkthrough ▶
16#1004Max Consecutive Ones IIIMedium

Given a list nums of 0s and 1s and an integer k, you may flip at most k of the 0s into 1s. Return the length of the longest run of consecutive 1s you can have after flipping. Example: [1,0,1,1,0,1] with k=1 → flip the 0 at index 1 to get 1,1,1,1 followed by 0,1 → longest run is 4.

Example input[1,0,1,1,0,1], k=1
→
Output4

Why? k=1 on [1,0,1,1,0,1]: flip the second 0 and positions 2–5 become 1,1,1,1 → length 4.

💡 Key idea Keep a window with at most k zeros. Grow on the right; shrink on the left when there are too many zeros.

Edge / failure case [0,0,0],0 → 0
No flips allowed and no 1s, so the longest run of 1s is 0.

Open walkthrough ▶
17#1493Longest Subarray of 1's After Deleting One ElementMedium

Given a binary list nums (0s and 1s), you MUST delete exactly one element. Return the length of the longest run of consecutive 1s that remains in the resulting list. Return 0 if there is no 1 at all. Example: [0,1,1,1,0,1,1,0,1] → delete the 0 at index 4 to join the 1,1,1 and 1,1 into a run of length 5.

Example input[0,1,1,1,0,1,1,0,1]
→
Output5

Why? [0,1,1,1,0,1,1,0,1]: delete the middle 0 (index 4). The 1,1,1 and 1,1 join into a run of 5.

💡 Key idea Keep a window with at most one zero. The answer is the window size minus 1 (the deleted spot).

Edge / failure case [1,1,1] → 2
All 1s — you still must delete one, so the run shrinks to 2.

Open walkthrough ▶
18#1732Find the Highest AltitudeEasy

A biker starts at altitude 0. The list gain has n numbers; gain[i] is the change in altitude between point i and point i+1 (positive = climbs, negative = descends). Return the highest altitude reached at any point, including the starting 0. Example: gain=[-5,1,5,0,-7] → altitudes 0, -5, -4, 1, 1, -6, so the highest is 1.

Example inputgain = [-5, 1, 5, 0, -7]
→
Output1

Why? 0 → -5 → -4 → 1 → 1 → -6. The highest height is 1.

💡 Key idea Keep a running total. Remember the biggest total seen.

Edge / failure case [-4,-3,-2,-1,4,3,2] → 0
The biker never climbs above the starting altitude 0.

19#724Find Pivot IndexEasy

Given a list nums, find the pivot index: the position where the sum of all numbers strictly to its left equals the sum of all numbers strictly to its right (the pivot number itself isn't counted; an empty side sums to 0). Return the LEFTMOST such index, or -1 if none exists. Example: [1,7,3,6,5,6] → at index 3 the left (1+7+3=11) equals the right (5+6=11), so 3.

Example input[1, 7, 3, 6, 5, 6]
→
Output3

Why? [1,7,3,6,5,6]: left of index 3 is 1+7+3 = 11. Right of it is 5+6 = 11. Equal → index 3.

💡 Key idea right sum = total − left sum − current number.

Edge / failure case [1,2,3] → -1
No index has equal sums on both sides → -1.

Open walkthrough ▶
20#2215Find the Difference of Two ArraysEasy

Given two lists nums1 and nums2, return two lists: first, all distinct numbers that appear in nums1 but not in nums2; second, all distinct numbers that appear in nums2 but not in nums1. Each number appears once in its list; order doesn't matter. Example: nums1=[1,2,3,3], nums2=[1,1,2,2] → [[3],[]] because only 3 is exclusive to nums1 and nothing is exclusive to nums2.

Example input[1,2,3,3], [1,1,2,2]
→
Output[[3], []]

Why? First list {1,2,3}, second {1,2}: only 3 is exclusive to the first, nothing exclusive to the second → [[3], []].

💡 Key idea Turn each list into a set, then subtract one from the other.

Edge / failure case [1,2,3],[2,4,6] → [[1, 3], [4, 6]]
1,3 only in nums1; 4,6 only in nums2; 2 is in both.

Open walkthrough ▶
21#1207Unique Number of OccurrencesEasy

Given a list arr, count how many times each distinct value occurs. Return true if all of those counts are different from one another, otherwise false. Example: [1,2,2,1,1,3] → 1 occurs 3 times, 2 occurs 2 times, 3 occurs once → counts 3,2,1 are all different → true.

Example input[1,2,2,1,1,3]
→
OutputTrue

Why? 1 appears 3 times, 2 appears 2 times, 3 appears once. The counts 3, 2, 1 are all different → True.

💡 Key idea Use a Counter, then check that the counts contain no duplicates.

Edge / failure case [1,2] → False
Both numbers occur exactly once — the counts are not unique → False.

22#1657Determine if Two Strings Are CloseMedium

Two strings are 'close' if you can turn one into the other using these operations any number of times: (1) swap any two characters' positions; (2) pick two existing letters and swap their roles everywhere (every a becomes b and every b becomes a). Given word1 and word2, return true if they are close. Example: 'cabbba' → 'abbccc' works: both have the same set of letters, and the counts (1,2,3) can be reassigned between letters.

Example input"cabbba", "abbccc"
→
OutputTrue

Why? cabbba has a×2, b×3, c×1. abbccc has a×1, b×2, c×3. Same letters, and the counts {1,2,3} match → True.

💡 Key idea They need the same set of letters and the same list of counts (in any order).

Edge / failure case 'a','aa' → False
Different lengths can never be made equal → False.

23#2352Equal Row and Column PairsMedium

grid is an n × n grid of integers. A row Ri and a column Cj form an equal pair if they contain the same numbers in the same order. Return the number of (row, column) pairs that are equal. Example: grid=[[3,2,1],[1,7,6],[2,7,7]] → row 2 is [2,7,7] and column 1 reads [2,7,7], so there is exactly 1 pair.

Example input[[3,2,1],[1,7,6],[2,7,7]]
→
Output1

Why? Row 3 is [2,7,7]. Column 2 reads down as 2,7,7. Same → 1 pair.

💡 Key idea Store each row as a tuple in a counter. Look up every column.

Edge / failure case [[1,2],[3,4]] → 0
No row matches any column → 0 pairs.

24#2390Removing Stars From a StringMedium

Given a string s that contains letters and '*' characters: each '*' removes ITSELF and the closest non-star letter to its left. Perform all removals and return the remaining string. Example: 'leet**cod*e' → the first * removes t, the second removes e, the last removes d → 'lecoe'.

Example input"leet**cod*e"
→
Output"lecoe"

Why? leet** → the first * deletes t, the second deletes e → 'le'. Then 'cod*e': * deletes d → 'coe'. Result: lecoe.

💡 Key idea Use a stack. Push letters. Pop when you see *.

Edge / failure case 'erase*****' → ''
Five stars delete all five letters, leaving an empty string.

25#735Asteroid CollisionMedium

asteroids is a list of integers. The absolute value is an asteroid's size; the sign is its direction (positive = moving right, negative = moving left); all move at the same speed. When two asteroids meet, the smaller explodes; if equal in size both explode; asteroids moving in the same direction never meet. Return the asteroids left after all collisions. Example: [5,10,-5,-12] → -5 hits 10 and explodes; -12 destroys both 10 and 5 → [-12].

Example input[5, 10, -5, -12]
→
Output[-12]

Why? [5,10,-5,-12]: -5 hits 10 and explodes. -12 hits 10, then 5, and destroys both. Only -12 is left.

💡 Key idea Keep a stack of survivors. Collisions only happen with the top of the stack.

Edge / failure case [8,-8] → []
Equal sizes meeting head-on: both explode → nothing left.

26#394Decode StringMedium

The input s is a compressed (encoded) string using the rule k[encoded], meaning the text inside the brackets is repeated exactly k times. Brackets can be nested, and plain letters may sit anywhere. Return the decoded string. Example: '3[a2[c]]' → inner 2[c] = 'cc', so inside the outer bracket we have 'acc'; repeat it 3 times → 'accaccacc'.

Example input"3[a2[c]]"
→
Output"accaccacc"

Why? 3[a2[c]]: 2[c] is cc, so a2[c] is acc, and 3 times that is accaccacc.

💡 Key idea A stack remembers the text so far and the repeat count each time you see '['.

Edge / failure case '2[abc]3[cd]ef' → 'abcabccdcdcdef'
Several blocks in a row each expand separately.

Open walkthrough ▶
27#933Number of Recent CallsEasy

Design a class RecentCounter. Its method ping(t) records a new request at time t (in milliseconds; each call has a larger t than the previous one) and returns how many requests happened in the time window [t − 3000, t], counting this one. Example: pings at 1, 100, 3001, 3002 return 1, 2, 3, 3 — at 3002 the window is [2, 3002], which no longer includes time 1.

Example inputping(1), ping(100), ping(3001), ping(3002)
→
Output1, 2, 3, 3

Why? Pings at 1 and 100 → 2. At 3001 the window is 1…3001, so 3. At 3002 the window is 2…3002, which drops the ping at 1 → still 3.

💡 Key idea Keep a queue of times. Remove old ones from the front.

Edge / failure case ping(1), ping(5000) → [1, 1]
The second ping is far outside the 3000 ms window of the first, so the count resets to 1.

Open walkthrough ▶
28#649Dota2 SenateMedium

The Senate has senators from two parties, Radiant (R) and Dire (D); the string senate lists them in voting order. Rounds repeat in that order. On his turn a senator either (a) bans one senator of the OTHER party from voting for the rest of the game, or (b) announces victory if all senators still allowed to vote belong to his own party. Every senator plays in the best way for his own party. Return 'Radiant' or 'Dire', whichever party wins. Example: 'RDD' → the first R bans the first D, the second D bans R, and only D's remain → 'Dire'.

Example input"RDD"
→
Output"Dire"

Why? RDD: R (index 0) bans the first D. The other D (index 2) then bans R. Dire wins.

💡 Key idea Use two queues of positions. The earlier senator acts, then rejoins the line for the next round.

Edge / failure case 'RD' → 'Radiant'
R acts first and bans D, so Radiant wins.

29#2095Delete the Middle Node of a Linked ListMedium

You are given the head of a singly linked list. Delete the middle node and return the head. For a list of n nodes, the middle node is the one at index ⌊n/2⌋ (counting from 0); for example with 7 nodes it's the 4th node, with 2 nodes it's the 2nd. Example: 1→3→4→7→1→2→6 → remove 7 → 1→3→4→1→2→6.

Example input1 → 3 → 4 → 7 → 1 → 2 → 6
→
Output1 → 3 → 4 → 1 → 2 → 6

Why? 7 nodes: the middle is the 4th (value 7). Remove it.

💡 Key idea Slow and fast pointers: when fast reaches the end, slow is at the middle.

Edge / failure case [2,1] → 2
With 2 nodes the middle is index 1, so only the first node remains.

30#328Odd Even Linked ListMedium

Given the head of a singly linked list, group all nodes at ODD positions (1st, 3rd, 5th … counting from 1) first, followed by all nodes at EVEN positions, keeping the original relative order inside each group. Do it in place with O(1) extra space and O(n) (time grows only in step with the input size) time, and return the head. Example: 1→2→3→4→5 → 1→3→5→2→4.

Example input1 → 2 → 3 → 4 → 5
→
Output1 → 3 → 5 → 2 → 4

Why? 1,2,3,4,5 → odd positions 1,3,5 first, then even positions 2,4 → 1,3,5,2,4.

💡 Key idea Build two chains (odd and even) and join them.

Edge / failure case [2,1,3,5,6,4,7] → 2 → 3 → 6 → 7 → 1 → 5 → 4
Odd positions (2,3,6,7) come first, then even positions (1,5,4).

Example input1 → 2 → 3 → 4 → 5
→
Output5 → 4 → 3 → 2 → 1

Why? 1→2→3→4→5 becomes 5→4→3→2→1.

💡 Key idea Walk along the list and flip each arrow to point backwards.

Edge / failure case [] → None

32#2130Maximum Twin Sum of a Linked ListMedium

In a linked list with an even number of nodes n, the node at index i (from 0) and the node at index n−1−i are twins, for 0 ≤ i < n/2. The twin sum is the sum of the two values. Return the maximum twin sum. Example: 4→2→2→3 → twins are (4,3) and (2,2), sums 7 and 4, so the answer is 7.

Example input4 → 2 → 2 → 3
→
Output7

Why? 4→2→2→3: twins are (4,3) = 7 and (2,2) = 4. The biggest is 7.

💡 Key idea Copy the values to an array and pair them from both ends.

Edge / failure case [1,100000] → 100001
With only two nodes they are twins of each other.

33#104Maximum Depth of Binary TreeEasy

Given the root of a binary tree, return its maximum depth: the number of NODES along the longest path from the root down to the farthest leaf. An empty tree has depth 0. Example: [3,9,20,null,null,15,7] → the path 3 → 20 → 15 has 3 nodes, so the answer is 3. (A tree written as a list reads level by level, top to bottom and left to right; null means there is no node in that spot.)

Example input[3, 9, 20, null, null, 15, 7]
→
Output3

Why? 3 → 20 → 15 passes through 3 nodes → depth 3.

💡 Key idea depth = 1 + the deeper of the left and right subtrees.

Edge / failure case [] → 0
An empty tree has depth 0.

34#872Leaf-Similar TreesEasy

The leaf value sequence of a binary tree is the list of its leaves (nodes with no children) read from left to right. Given two trees root1 and root2, return true if their leaf value sequences are identical, even if the trees have different shapes. Example: both trees have leaves 6, 7, 4, 9, 8 in that order → true.

Example inputtwo trees with leaves 6,7,4,9,8
→
OutputTrue

Why? Both trees give 6, 7, 4, 9, 8 → True.

💡 Key idea Collect leaves with DFS, then compare the two lists.

Edge / failure case [1,2,3],[1,3,2] → False
Leaves read 2,3 in one tree and 3,2 in the other → not similar.

35#1448Count Good Nodes in Binary TreeMedium

In a binary tree (boxes called nodes, each with up to two children), call a node 'good' if, on the path from the top node (root) down to it, no earlier node has a value GREATER than it. The root itself is always good. Given the root, return how many good nodes there are. Example: [3,1,4,3,null,1,5] → the good nodes are 3 (root), 4, 3 (below the 1, nothing on its path is bigger) and 5 → 4. (A tree written as a list reads level by level, top to bottom and left to right; null means there is no node in that spot.)

Example input[3, 1, 4, 3, null, 1, 5]
→
Output4

Why? 3 (root) good. 1 isn't (3 is bigger). The next 3 is good (3 ≥ 3). 4 is good. 1 isn't (4 is bigger). 5 is good → 4.

💡 Key idea Pass the biggest value seen so far down the path.

Edge / failure case [3,3,null,4,2] → 3
The node 2 is smaller than 3 and 4 above it, so it is not good.

The path's length is the number of nodes visited minus 1 (the number of moves). Given the root, return the length of the longest ZigZag path anywhere in the tree. Example: the path root → right child → left child → right child makes 3 moves, so the answer is 3.

Example input1 → R 2 → L 3 → R 4 (and 2 → R 5)
→
Output3

💡 Key idea Pass down the last direction and the length. Switching direction adds 1; same direction restarts at 1.

A single element is the answer.

1#88Merge Sorted ArrayEasy

You have two lists of numbers, each already sorted from smallest to largest. nums1 has extra empty slots (shown as 0) at its end, just enough to hold all the numbers of nums2; m and n say how many REAL numbers each list has. Merge nums2 into nums1 so that nums1 ends up as one sorted list, by changing nums1 itself (don't make a new list). Example: nums1=[1,2,3,0,0,0] (m=3), nums2=[2,5,6] (n=3) → nums1 becomes [1,2,2,3,5,6].

Example inputnums1=[1,2,3,0,0,0], m=3, nums2=[2,5,6], n=3
→
Output[1,2,2,3,5,6]

Why? nums1 = [1,2,3,_,_,_], nums2 = [2,5,6] → [1,2,2,3,5,6].

💡 Key idea Fill nums1 from the BACK with the biggest remaining value, so nothing gets overwritten.

Edge / failure case nums1=[1], m=1, nums2=[], n=0 → [1]
nums2 is empty, so nums1 stays as it is.

2#27Remove ElementEasy

You have a list of numbers nums and a number val. Remove every copy of val from the list by changing the list itself (not making a new one): move all the other numbers to the front, in any order you like. Return k, how many numbers are left. Only the first k slots are checked; what sits after them doesn't matter. Example: nums=[3,2,2,3], val=3 → k=2 and the list starts with [2,2].

Example inputnums=[3,2,2,3], val=3
→
Output2 → [2,2,_,_]

Why? [3,2,2,3], val = 3 → keep 2,2 → return 2.

💡 Key idea One pointer reads, another writes: copy only the values you want to keep.

Edge / failure case [0,1,2,2,3,0,4,2], val=2 → [5, [0, 0, 1, 3, 4]]
Every 2 is removed; the other 5 numbers stay.

Example: [0,0,1,1,1,2,2,3,3,4] → k=5 and the list starts [0,1,2,3,4].

→
Output5 → [0,1,2,3,4,…]

Why? [0,0,1,1,1,2,2,3,3,4] → 0,1,2,3,4 → return 5.

💡 Key idea Compare each number with the last kept one; keep it only if different.

Edge / failure case [1,2,3] → [3, [1, 2, 3]]
No duplicates, so nothing is removed.

Same as the previous problem, but each number may stay up to TWICE. →

→

Output2

→

1#8String to Integer (atoi)Medium

Convert a piece of text s into a whole number, the way a simple calculator would read it. Step 1: skip any spaces at the start. Step 2: if the next character is '+' or '-', that is the sign (no sign means positive). Step 3: read digits until you hit something that is not a digit, then stop. If there were no digits, the answer is 0. If the number is outside the 32-bit range (−2,147,483,648 to 2,147,483,647), return the nearest limit. Example: ' -042' → spaces skipped, sign '-', digits 042 → -42.

Example input" -042"
→
Output-42

Why? Turn text like ' -42abc' into the number -42: skip spaces, read a sign, read digits, stop at anything else.

💡 Key idea Walk left to right once with an index: spaces → optional sign → digits. Clamp to the 32-bit range at the end.

Edge / failure case 'words and 987' → 0
The text starts with a letter, not a digit, so no number is read at all and the answer is 0.

2#438Find All Anagrams in a StringMedium

Given two strings s and p, return the starting positions of every piece of s (letters sitting next to each other) that is an anagram of p — a rearrangement of exactly the same letters, using each the same number of times. Return the positions in any order. Example: s='cbaebabacd', p='abc' → 'cba' starts at 0 and 'bac' starts at 6 → [0, 6].

Example inputs="cbaebabacd", p="abc"
→
Output[0, 6]

Why? Where in the text does a rearrangement of the word p appear? 'cbaebabacd' with p='abc' → starts at 0 ('cba') and 6 ('bac').

💡 Key idea Slide a window the same length as p. Keep letter counts for the window and for p; when they match, record the start. Each step adds one letter and drops one.

Edge / failure case s="abab", p="ab" → [0, 1, 2]
Windows overlap: 'ab' at 0, 'ba' at 1 and 'ab' at 2 are all rearrangements of 'ab'.

Given two strings s1 and s2, return true if some piece of s2 (letters sitting next to each other) is a rearrangement (permutation) of all the letters of s1, otherwise false. Example: s1='ab', s2='eidbaooo' → the piece 'ba' is a rearrangement of 'ab' → true. With s2='eidboaoo' → false.

Example inputs1="ab", s2="eidbaooo"
→
OutputTrue

Why? Does the text contain some rearrangement of s1 as a continuous piece? s1='ab', s2='eidbaooo' → 'ba' is there → True.

💡 Key idea Slide a window the same length as s1 over s2 and compare letter counts; a match means a rearrangement is hiding there.

Edge / failure case s1="ab", s2="eidboaoo" → False
'b' and 'a' are never next to each other, so no window of length 2 is a rearrangement of 'ab'.

You have a list of words, each exactly two lowercase letters. You may pick some of them (each at most once) and join them in any order into one string. Return the length of the LONGEST palindrome (a string that reads the same backwards) you can build, or 0 if none. Example: ['lc','cl','gg'] → 'lc' + 'gg' + 'cl' = 'lcggcl', which reads the same both ways → 6.

Example input["lc", "cl", "gg"]
→
Output6

Why? Join some of the given 2-letter words (each at most once) into the longest palindrome. 'lc' and 'cl' pair up; 'gg' can sit in the middle.

💡 Key idea A word pairs with its reverse (each pair adds 4 letters). A word like 'gg' pairs with another 'gg', and one leftover 'gg' can be the centre (+2).

Edge / failure case ["cc", "ll", "xx"] → 2
No word has a partner, but each is its own reverse; only one can sit in the middle, so the length is 2.

5#742Closest Leaf in a Binary TreeMedium

You are given a binary tree (boxes called nodes, each with up to two children; all values are different) and a value k that belongs to one of its nodes. A leaf is a node with no children. Return the value of the leaf that is NEAREST to the node with value k, where distance counts the steps (edges) along the tree, going up or down. Example tree [1,2,3,4,null,null,null,5,null,6] with k=2: leaf 3 is 2 steps away (2 → 1 → 3), leaf 6 is 3 steps away (2 → 4 → 5 → 6) → 3. (A tree written as a list reads level by level, top to bottom and left to right; null means there is no node in that spot.)

Example inputtree [1,2,3,4,null,null,null,5,null,6], k=2
→
Output3

Why? Starting at the node with value k, which leaf (a node with no children) is the fewest steps away — going up, down, any direction?

💡 Key idea A tree only lets you walk down, but the answer may be up and over. Turn the tree into an undirected graph (add parent links) and run a BFS from k until you hit a leaf.

Node 3 is itself a leaf, so the answer is 3 without moving.

You are given a binary tree (boxes called nodes with up to two children, all values different) and two values: startValue and destValue (both present). Example: tree [5,1,2,3,null,6,4], start 3, dest 6 → go up from 3 to 1, up to 5, then right to 2 and left to 6 → 'UURL'. (A tree written as a list reads level by level, top to bottom and left to right; null means there is no node in that spot.)

Example inputtree [5,1,2,3,null,6,4], start=3, dest=6
→
Output"UURL"

💡 Key idea Find the path from the root to each node as letters L/R. The shared beginning of both paths is the common route — cut it off. Each leftover letter of A's path becomes a U; B's leftover letters stay as they are.

→
Output4

Why? A grid of 0 (open) and 1 (blocked). Walk from the top-left to the bottom-right, moving in 8 directions through 0s. How many squares is the shortest path?

→
Output2

Why? Walk from the start square to the end square stepping on EVERY empty square exactly once. Count how many such walks exist.

💡 Key idea Try all four moves from each square, marking it used and un-marking on the way back (backtracking). Count a path only if it reaches the end with no empty squares left.

An IP address like 255.0.0.7 is really a 32-bit number. Example: ip='255.0.0.7', n=10 → ['255.0.0.7/32', '255.0.0.8/29', '255.0.0.16/32'] (1 + 8 + 1 = 10 addresses).

Example inputip="255.0.0.7", n=10
→
Output["255.0.0.7/32", "255.0.0.8/29", "255.0.0.16/32"]

Why? Cover exactly n consecutive IP addresses with as few 'CIDR blocks' as possible. A block starting at an address can only be as big as that address's lowest set bit allows.

💡 Key idea Treat the IP as a 32-bit number. At each start, take the biggest block that is both aligned (lowest set bit) and not larger than what is left (n). Write it as ip/(32−log2 size).

A single address needs one block of size 1, written /32.

→
Output3, 4, 3

16#900RLE IteratorMedium

A long list of numbers is stored in run-length form: encoding = [count1, value1, count2, value2, …] means count1 copies of value1, then count2 copies of value2, and so on. Build an iterator over it. next(n) 'uses up' the next n numbers and returns the LAST number used, or -1 if fewer than n numbers remain. Example: encoding [3,8,0,9,2,5] stands for 8,8,8,5,5. next(2) → 8, next(1) → 8, next(1) → 5, next(2) → -1 (only one 5 is left).

Example inputRLEIterator([3,8,0,9,2,5]); next(2), next(1), next(1), next(2)
→
Output8, 8, 5, -1

Why? A long list is stored compressed as pairs: [3,8] means three 8s. next(n) skips n items and returns the last one skipped, or −1 if there aren't enough.

💡 Key idea Never expand the list. Keep a pointer to the current pair and spend the requested n against that pair's remaining count, moving on when a pair is used up.

Edge / failure case RLEIterator([2,4]); next(1), next(3), next(1) → [4, -1, -1]
Only two 4s exist. Asking for 3 more runs past the end, so it returns -1, and everything is used up.

→
Outputbar, bar, bar2, bar2

Output2

Why? A list of n+1 numbers uses only the values 1..n, so one value repeats. Find it without changing the list or using extra memory. [1,3,4,2,2] → 2.

💡 Key idea Treat each value as a pointer to the next position: the repeat creates a loop. A slow and a fast pointer meet inside the loop; restarting one from the start and moving both one step at a time makes them meet at the loop entrance — the duplicate.

Edge / failure case [3, 1, 3, 4, 2] → 3
The repeated value 3 appears at the very start and in the middle; the loop-finding steps still land on it.

→
Output2

Example: 3 people where everybody knows person 1 and person 1 knows nobody → 1.

Example input3 people; everybody knows person 1, who knows nobody
→
Output1

Why? At a party a celebrity is known by everybody but knows nobody. You can only ask 'does A know B?'. Find the celebrity with as few questions as you can.

💡 Key idea Each question removes one person: if the candidate knows i, the candidate cannot be the celebrity (i is the new candidate); otherwise i cannot be. After one pass check the single survivor against everyone.

Edge / failure case 3 people in a ring of 'knows' → -1
Everyone knows someone, so nobody is known-by-all and knows-nobody: there is no celebrity (-1).

Open walkthrough ▶
41#490The MazeMedium

A ball rolls in a maze. The maze is a grid where 0 is an empty square and 1 is a wall. When you push the ball in a direction (up, down, left or right) it keeps rolling until it hits a wall (or the edge of the maze), and only then stops; you may then push it again. Given the start square and the destination square, return true if the ball can STOP exactly on the destination, otherwise false. Example: the 5 × 5 maze with start (0,4) and destination (4,4) → true.

Example input5×5 maze, start (0,4), destination (4,4)
→
OutputTrue

Why? A ball rolls in a direction until it hits a wall. Can it STOP exactly on the destination?

💡 Key idea The ball's decisions only happen where it stops. Treat each stopping square as a graph node; from it, roll in 4 directions to find the next stopping squares, and search (BFS) for the destination.

Edge / failure case same maze, destination (3,2) → False
The ball can roll THROUGH (3,2) but cannot stop there (it keeps rolling until a wall), so the destination is never a stopping point: False.

A network has n nodes labelled 1 to n. times[i] = [u, v, w] means a signal sent from u arrives at v after w time units (one direction only). A signal is sent from node k. Return the time it takes for ALL n nodes to receive it, or -1 if some node never receives it. Example: n=4, k=2, links 2→1 (1), 2→3 (1), 3→4 (1) → node 4 gets it at time 2, which is the last → 2.

Example inputtimes [[2,1,1],[2,3,1],[3,4,1]], n=4, k=2
→
Output2

Why? A signal starts at node k and travels along one-way links, each with a travel time. How long until EVERY node has it (or −1 if some never do)?

💡 Key idea Dijkstra: always settle the unvisited node with the smallest arrival time using a min-heap. The answer is the largest arrival time, if all n nodes were reached.

Node 1 has no outgoing link, so the signal never reaches node 2: -1.

A 2 × 3 board holds the tiles 1 to 5 and one empty square written 0. A move swaps the empty square with a tile next to it (up, down, left or right). The puzzle is solved when the board is [[1,2,3],[4,5,0]]. Given the starting board, return the fewest moves needed to solve it, or -1 if it can't be solved. Example: [[4,1,2],[5,0,3]] → 5 moves.

Example input[[4,1,2],[5,0,3]]
→
Output5

Why? A 2×3 puzzle with tiles 1–5 and one empty square. Slide tiles into the gap to reach [[1,2,3],[4,5,0]]. Fewest moves?

💡 Key idea Every arrangement of the board is a state; one slide gets you to a neighbouring state. BFS from the start state finds the fewest moves; if the goal is never reached, it is unsolvable (−1).

You have a directed graph of n nodes (0 to n−1) where each node has at most ONE outgoing edge: edges[i] is the node that i points to, or -1 if it points nowhere. A cycle is a path that returns to where it started. Return the length of the LONGEST cycle (the number of nodes in it), or -1 if there is none. Example: edges [3,3,4,2,3] → the nodes 2 → 4 → 3 → 2 form a cycle of length 3 → 3.

Example inputedges [3, 3, 4, 2, 3]
→
Output3

Why? Every node points to at most one other node. What is the length of the longest cycle (−1 if none)?

💡 Key idea Follow pointers from each unvisited node, stamping each node with a step number. If you reach a node stamped during THIS walk, the difference of stamps is the cycle length.

The pointers lead to the dead end (-1) and never loop, so there is no cycle: -1.

→
Output1

Why? Repeatedly smash the two heaviest stones: equal stones vanish, otherwise the lighter one vanishes and the heavier loses that much weight. What remains?

💡 Key idea You always need the two largest stones, which is exactly what a max-heap gives (Python's heapq is a min-heap, so store negatives).

Edge / failure case [1, 1] → 0

→
Output12

Why? k times: pick a pile and remove half of it (rounded down). Make the total as small as possible.

💡 Key idea Removing half of the BIGGEST pile always saves the most, so keep the piles in a max-heap and halve the top k times.

Halving a pile of 1 removes 0 stones (rounded down), so no operation changes anything.

→
Output8

→

58#2539Count the Number of Good SubsequencesHard

A subsequence of a string is what you get by deleting some letters (possibly none) without changing the order of the rest; different sets of positions count as different subsequences. A non-empty subsequence is 'good' if every letter that appears in it appears the SAME number of times. Given a string s, return how many good subsequences it has, modulo 1,000,000,007 (i.e. the remainder after dividing by that number). Example: 'aabb' → 11.

Example input"aabb"
→
Output11

Why? A subsequence is 'good' if every letter in it appears the same number of times. Count the good subsequences (answer mod 1,000,000,007).

💡 Key idea Fix a frequency f. For each letter you either skip it or choose f of its c copies (C(c, f) ways): multiply (1 + C(c, f)) over all letters, subtract the empty choice, and add up over every f.

Edge / failure case "aaa" → 7
With one distinct letter, every non-empty selection of positions is good: 2³ − 1 = 7.

Design a circular queue of fixed size k: a line where you add at the back and remove from the front, and when the end of its storage is reached it wraps around to reuse the freed space at the beginning. Example: size 3 → enQueue 1, 2, 3 succeed, enQueue 4 fails (full); Rear() = 3; deQueue() frees one slot; enQueue 4 now works and Rear() = 4.

→
OutputTrue,True,True,False,3,True,True,True,4

Why? A queue of fixed size that reuses freed space at the front by wrapping around the end of its array.

💡 Key idea Store items in an array. The front is at index head; the next free slot is (head + size) % capacity. Use modulo to wrap.

Nothing was added: Front reports -1, deQueue has nothing to remove (False) and the queue is empty (True).

A class FizzBuzz(n) has four methods, each run by its own thread at the same time: fizz(printFizz), buzz(printBuzz), fizzbuzz(printFizzBuzz) and number(printNumber). Together the four threads must output the numbers 1 to n in order, but each position is printed by only ONE thread: 'Fizz' for multiples of 3 only, 'Buzz' for multiples of 5 only, 'FizzBuzz' for multiples of both, and the plain number for the rest. 💡 Key idea Share a counter i. Each thread waits until i is a number it is responsible for, prints, increases i and wakes the others. A condition variable (wait / notify_all) coordinates the turns.

With n = 3 only the first three numbers are produced: 1, 2, fizz. All four threads still stop cleanly.

1#31Next PermutationMedium

A permutation is one arrangement of a list of numbers. If you list all arrangements of the same numbers in dictionary order (compare the first position where two arrangements differ), the 'next permutation' is the one that comes right after the current one. Given the list nums, change it IN PLACE into its next permutation; if it is already the very last arrangement, change it into the first (smallest) one. Example: [1,2,3] → [1,3,2]; [3,2,1] → [1,2,3].

Example input[1, 2, 3]
→
Output[1, 3, 2]

Why? Rearrange the list into the next bigger arrangement (like the next word in a dictionary); if it is already the biggest, make it the smallest. [1,2,3] → [1,3,2].

💡 Key idea From the right, find the first number smaller than its right neighbour (the dip). Swap it with the next-larger number to its right, then reverse everything after the dip so the tail is as small as possible.

Edge / failure case [3, 2, 1] → [1, 2, 3]
[3,2,1] is already the largest arrangement, so the next one wraps around to the smallest: [1,2,3].

You are given a string s of capital letters and a number k. You may change at most k letters in s to any other capital letter. Return the length of the longest stretch of identical letters (letters sitting next to each other) you can make. Example: s='AABABBA', k=1 → change one B in 'AABA' to A to get 'AAAA' → 4.

Example inputs="AABABBA", k=1
→
Output4

Why? You may change at most k letters. How long a stretch of identical letters can you make? 'AABABBA', k=1 → 4.

💡 Key idea A window is fixable if (window length − count of its most common letter) ≤ k. Slide a window, keep the largest letter count seen, and shrink by one when the window needs too many changes.

No changes are allowed and all letters differ, so the longest run of one letter is 1.

4#992Subarrays with K Different IntegersHard

Given a list nums of positive numbers and a number k, return the number of 'good' runs: runs of numbers sitting next to each other (subarrays) that contain EXACTLY k different values. Example: [1,2,1,2,3], k=2 → [1,2], [2,1], [1,2], [2,3], [1,2,1], [2,1,2], [1,2,1,2] → 7.

→
Output7

You are given the root of a binary tree (boxes called nodes, each with up to two children, all values different) and the value start of one node. At minute 0 only that node is infected. Every minute, each uninfected node that is next to an infected node (its parent or one of its children) becomes infected. Return the number of minutes until the whole tree is infected. Example: tree [1,5,3,null,4,10,6,9,2], start 3 → 4 minutes. (A tree written as a list reads level by level, top to bottom and left to right; null means there is no node in that spot.)

Example inputtree [1,5,3,null,4,10,6,9,2], start 3
→
Output4

Why? An infection starts at one node and spreads to each neighbour (children and parent) every minute. How many minutes until the whole tree is infected?

💡 Key idea Turn the tree into an undirected graph by adding parent links, then BFS from the start node; the number of BFS levels (minus one) is the time.

Edge / failure case tree [1], start 1 → 0
Only one node, already infected at minute 0.

Example: n=4, cables [[0,1],[1,2],[2,0],[1,3]] → only the cable 1–3 is critical, because server 3 hangs on it alone → [[1,3]].

Example inputn=4, [[0,1],[1,2],[2,0],[1,3]]
→
Output[[1,3]]

Why? Which cables, if cut, would split the network into two parts (the 'bridges')?

💡 Key idea DFS and number nodes in visit order (disc). low[u] is the earliest visited node reachable from u's subtree without using the edge to its parent. An edge u–v is a bridge when low[v] > disc[u] — v's side cannot get back around.

Edge / failure case a triangle of 3 servers → []
The three cables form a triangle: cutting any one still leaves the others connected, so there are no critical connections.

💡 Key idea Join the middle (centre) of each tree. The result's diameter is max(d1, d2, ⌈d1/2⌉ + ⌈d2/2⌉ + 1), where d1 and d2 are the two diameters (found with two BFS passes each).

Two single-node trees joined by one edge give a path of length 1.

→

💡 Key idea The allowed flips walk through a Gray-code sequence, so the answer is the position of n in that sequence: the XOR of n with all its right-shifts (n ^ n>>1 ^ n>>2 ^ …).

20#3953Maximum Score With Co-Prime ElementHard

You are given a list nums and a number maxVal. You may change any element to any positive whole number up to maxVal; each change costs 1. Two numbers are co-prime if their only common divisor is 1. After the changes, you must pick one index i whose value is co-prime with EVERY other element. The score is (the final value of nums[i]) minus (the total number of elements you changed). Return the maximum possible score. Example: nums [3,4,6], maxVal 5 → change 6 to 5 (cost 1) and pick it: 5 is co-prime with 3 and 4 → score 5 − 1 = 4.

Example inputnums [3, 4, 6], maxVal=5
→
Output4

Why? Pick one element and make it co-prime with all others. You may change values (each change costs 1) to anything up to maxVal. Score = chosen value − total cost.

💡 Key idea For every candidate final value v count how many numbers are NOT co-prime with it (inclusion–exclusion with the Möbius function over multiples). Those must each be changed (to 1); one extra change may turn some element into v. Take the best v − cost.

Edge / failure case nums [2, 4], maxVal=4 → 3
2 and 4 share a factor; the best is to keep one of them and change the other to 1.

23#460LFU CacheHard

Design a cache (a small fast memory) with a fixed capacity that evicts the Least Frequently Used key when it is full; if several keys are tied for least frequently used, evict the one that was used longest ago. get(key) returns the value or -1; put(key, value) stores a value. Both calls count as 'using' the key. Aim for constant time per call. Example (capacity 2): put(1,1), put(2,2), get(1) → 1, put(3,3) evicts key 2, get(2) → -1, get(3) → 3, put(4,4) evicts key 1, get(1) → -1, get(3) → 3, get(4) → 4.

Example inputcapacity 2: put 1, put 2, get 1, put 3, get 2, get 3, put 4, get 1, get 3, get 4
→
Output1, -1, 3, -1, 3, 4

Why? A cache with a fixed size. When full, throw out the Least Frequently Used key; if tied, the one used longest ago.

💡 Key idea Keep each key's use-count, group keys into buckets by count (each bucket remembers order), and track the smallest count. Evict from the front of the smallest bucket; a use moves a key to the next bucket.

Edge / failure case capacity 0: put(1,1), get(1) → [None, -1]
Capacity 0 means nothing can ever be stored, so get returns -1.

→

Why? Reverse only the letters of a string; every other character stays put. 'a-bC-dEf-ghIj' → 'j-Ih-gfE-dCba'.

→

Why? Build the target letter by letter, taking each letter from a column of the dictionary words; columns must go left to right and once you use column j you can't go back.

💡 Key idea Count, for each column, how many words have each letter. dp[t] = ways to build the first t target letters using columns so far; each column adds dp[t−1] × (count of target[t−1] in that column).

Edge / failure case words [abc], target "abcd" → 0
The target (4 letters) is longer than the words (3 columns), so it cannot be built at all.

events[i] = [start, end, value]. You may attend at most k events. Return the largest total value you can collect. Example: [[1,2,4],[3,4,3],[2,3,1]], k=2 → attend the first (value 4, days 1–2) and the second (value 3, days 3–4) → 7.

→
Output7

Why? Each event has a start, an end and a value. Attend at most k non-overlapping events to maximise the total value.

💡 Key idea Sort by start. For event i you either skip it or take it and jump to the first event starting after it ends (binary search), with one fewer event allowed. Cache by (i, left).

Two painters paint n walls. The paid painter paints wall i in time[i] time units and charges cost[i]. The free painter paints any wall in 1 time unit at no cost, but can only work while the paid painter is busy. Return the minimum total cost to paint all walls. Example: cost [1,2,3,2], time [1,2,3,2] → pay for walls 0 and 1 (cost 3, 3 time units): the free painter paints the other two meanwhile → 3.

Example inputcost [1,2,3,2], time [1,2,3,2]
→
Output3

Why? A paid painter paints wall i in time[i] and costs cost[i]; a free painter paints one wall per time unit but only while the paid painter is busy. Cheapest way to paint all walls?

💡 Key idea Hiring the paid painter for wall i effectively paints 1 + time[i] walls. So choose walls to pay for such that their (1 + time) sum covers n walls at minimum total cost — a knapsack.

Edge / failure case cost [2,3,4,2], time [1,1,1,1] → 4
Paying for two cheap walls (cost 2 + 2) lets the free painter cover the other two: total 4.

You are given a list nums and a number k. Return the length of the longest good subsequence. Example: nums [1,2,1,1,3], k=2 → the subsequence [1,2,1,1] has two changes (1→2 and 2→1) → length 4.

Example inputnums [1,2,1,1,3], k=2
→
Output4

Why? A subsequence is 'good' if at most k neighbouring pairs in it are different. Find the longest good subsequence.

💡 Key idea dp[i][j] = longest good subsequence ending at index i with j 'changes' (neighbouring pairs that differ). Extend from any earlier index p: same value costs no change, a different value costs one.

With no changes allowed all chosen numbers must be equal: the best is the two 1s.

42#3177Find the Maximum Length of a Good Subsequence IIHard

You are given a list nums and a number k. A subsequence (numbers picked in order, not necessarily next to each other) is 'good' if there are at most k positions where two neighbouring picked numbers are DIFFERENT. Return the length of the longest good subsequence; this version has a much longer list, so check every pair of positions is too slow. Example: nums [1,2,1,1,3], k=2 → [1,2,1,1] → 4.

Example inputnums [1,2,1,1,3], k=2
→
Output4

Why? Same as part I, but the list is much longer, so the O(n²) approach is too slow.

💡 Key idea For each value x and number of changes j keep the best length ending with x, and for each j the best over ALL values. A new element either continues a run of the same value (no change) or extends the best subsequence with j−1 changes.

Edge / failure case nums [1,2,3,4,5,1], k=0 → 2
With no changes allowed all chosen numbers must be equal: the best is the two 1s.

You are given a sorted list of positive numbers nums and a number n. You may add ('patch') extra numbers to the list. Example: nums=[1,3], n=6 → 1,3 and 1+3 = 4 are possible but 2 is not; add 2 and you can make 1..6 → 1 patch.

Example inputnums [1, 3], n=6
→
Output1

Why? Add the fewest numbers to a sorted list so that every value from 1 to n can be made as a sum of some of its numbers.

💡 Key idea Keep 'reach' = every value up to it is already buildable. If the next list number is ≤ reach+1 it extends reach for free; otherwise patch in reach+1, which doubles the range.

Edge / failure case nums [], n=7 → 3
Nothing to start with: patch 1, 2 and 4 to reach every value up to 7.

You are given a list of tasks, each a capital letter (the same letter means the same kind of task). Each task takes one unit of time, and the CPU may also sit idle for a unit. Example: ['A','A','A','B','B','B'] with n=2 → A B idle A B idle A B → 8.

Example inputA,A,A,B,B,B with n=2
→
Output8

Why? Run tasks (A–Z), one per time unit; the same task needs n units of cool-down between runs. Fewest time units to finish (idle allowed)?

💡 Key idea The most frequent task decides the frame: (maxCount−1) blocks of length n+1, plus the last row of all tasks tied at maxCount. If tasks exceed that, no idle is needed: the answer is at least len(tasks).

45#1057Campus BikesMedium

There are n workers and m bikes (m ≥ n) on a grid, all given as [x, y] points. Assign each worker a different bike by repeating this rule: among all unassigned worker–bike pairs choose the pair with the smallest Manhattan distance (|x1 − x2| + |y1 − y2|); if several pairs tie, pick the one with the smallest worker index, and if still tied the smallest bike index. Return a list where entry i is the bike given to worker i. Example: workers [[0,0],[2,1]], bikes [[1,2],[3,3]] → worker 1 takes bike 0 (distance 2), then worker 0 gets bike 1 → [1,0].

Example inputworkers [[0,0],[2,1]], bikes [[1,2],[3,3]]
→
Output[1, 0]

Why? Assign each worker a different bike: always take the (worker, bike) pair with the smallest walking distance; break ties by smaller worker, then smaller bike.

💡 Key idea List every worker–bike pair as (distance, worker, bike), sort them, and walk through: if both the worker and the bike are still free, make the assignment.

Edge / failure case three workers, three bikes with tied distances → [0, 2, 1]
Several pairs have equal distance; ties go to the smaller worker index, then the smaller bike index.

You are given a list nums sorted from smallest to largest. In one operation you pick two positions i < j with nums[i] < nums[j] and remove both numbers (the rest keep their order). You may repeat this any number of times, including zero. Return the smallest possible length of the list afterwards. Example: [1,3,4,9] → remove (1,3) and (4,9) → 0. Example input[1, 3, 4, 9]
→
Output0

Why? In a sorted list, repeatedly delete two numbers where the left one is strictly smaller than the right one. How short can the list get?

💡 Key idea If one value takes more than half of the list, it can only be paired with other values, leaving 2·maxCount − n of it behind. Otherwise everything pairs off and 0 or 1 remains (n odd).

Both numbers are equal, so neither is smaller than the other: nothing can be removed.

47#2851String TransformationHard

You are given two strings s and t of the same length and a number k. In one operation you take a suffix of s (the last l letters, 0 < l < n) and move it to the front (so 'abcd' with l=2 becomes 'cdab'). Return the number of different sequences of EXACTLY k operations that turn s into t, modulo 1,000,000,007. Two sequences are different if at some step they use different values of l. Example: s='abcd', t='cdab', k=2 → 2 ways.

Example inputs="abcd", t="cdab", k=2
→
Output2

Why? In one operation you move a suffix of s to the front (a rotation). In how many ways can you turn s into t using exactly k operations?

💡 Key idea Each operation rotates by 1..n−1 positions. Track the net rotation: from 'rotation 0' there are n−1 ways to leave; from any other rotation, one way returns to 0 and n−2 ways stay non-zero. A 2×2 matrix raised to the power k counts this fast; multiply by how many rotations of s equal t.

Edge / failure case s=t="ababab", k=1 → 2
The word equals itself after rotating by 2 or by 4 positions, so one operation can give the same word in 2 ways.

For each centre return the maximum number of servers that can be upgraded. Example: count [4,3], upgrade [3,5], sell [4,2], money [8,9] → [3,2].

→

There is so much money that every server can be upgraded: capped by the number of servers (2).

→
Outputsame list

Output1

Example input"abc"
→
Output3

Why? Count the pieces of a string that read the same backwards (each position counts separately). 'aaa' has 6.

💡 Key idea Every palindrome has a centre (a letter, or the gap between two). From each of the 2n−1 centres expand outwards while the letters match, counting each step.

Edge / failure case "aaa" → 6
Every single letter (3), every adjacent pair (2) and the whole word (1) is a palindrome: 6.

→

9#2817Minimum Absolute Difference Between Elements With ConstraintMedium

Given a list nums and a number x, find two numbers at positions i and j with |i − j| ≥ x whose values are as close as possible, and return the smallest absolute difference between their values. Example: nums [4,3,2,4], x=2 → positions 0 and 3 both hold 4, they are 3 apart (≥ 2) → difference 0.

Example inputnums [4, 3, 2, 4], x=2
→
Output0

Why? Find two numbers at least x positions apart whose values are as close as possible.

💡 Key idea Walk through the list; for position i, all elements at positions ≤ i − x are allowed partners. Keep them in a sorted list and use binary search to find the closest value to nums[i].

38#2043Simple Bank SystemMedium

Design a Bank for n accounts numbered 1 to n, created with the list of starting balances. transfer(account1, account2, money), deposit(account, money) and withdraw(account, money) each return true and change the balances if the action is valid, otherwise return false and change nothing. An action is invalid if any account number is not between 1 and n, or if money would be taken from an account that has less than that amount. Example: balances [10,100,20,50,30]: withdraw(3,10) → true; transfer(5,1,20) → true; deposit(5,20) → true; transfer(3,4,15) → false (account 3 has only 10); withdraw(10,50) → false (no such account).

Example inputBank([10,100,20,50,30]); withdraw(3,10), transfer(5,1,20), deposit(5,20), transfer(3,4,15), withdraw(10,50)
→
OutputTrue, True, True, False, False

Why? A bank with numbered accounts: transfer, deposit and withdraw. Each action succeeds only if the account numbers are valid and there is enough money.

💡 Key idea Keep balances in a list. Before each action check the account number is in range and (for money leaving an account) that the balance is enough; only then change the balances.

Edge / failure case deposit(0,5), transfer(1,9,1) → [False, False]
Account numbers run from 1 to n, so account 0 and account 9 do not exist: both actions fail.

39#3072Distribute Elements Into Two Arrays IIHard

You are given a list nums of different numbers. Build two lists arr1 and arr2: put the first number in arr1 and the second number in arr2. For every later number x, count how many numbers already in each list are strictly GREATER than x. Put x in arr1 if arr1 has the larger count and in arr2 if arr2 has the larger count; if the counts are equal put it in the list with fewer numbers, and if the lists have the same length put it in arr1. Return arr1 followed by arr2. Example: [2,1,3,3] → [2,3,1,3].

Example input[2, 1, 3, 3]
→
Output[2, 3, 1, 3]

Why? Deal numbers into two lists: the first to list 1, the second to list 2, then each next number goes to the list in which MORE elements are greater than it (ties: the shorter list, then list 1).

💡 Key idea You need 'how many elements already in a list are greater than x' fast. Compress the values to ranks and keep a Fenwick tree (binary indexed tree) per list that counts values; a prefix query gives the number ≤ x.

Edge / failure case [5, 14, 3, 1, 2] → [5, 3, 1, 2, 14]
The first two numbers start the lists; then each new number goes to the list with more elements greater than it, or the shorter list on a tie.

40#3161Block Placement QueriesHard

A number line starts at 0 and extends right. You process queries in order. [1, x] places an obstacle at position x (no two obstacles share a position). [2, x, sz] asks: can a block of length sz be placed somewhere in the part from 0 to x (the block must lie completely inside [0, x] and may touch obstacles only at its ends, not cover one)? Return the answers to the type-2 queries as a list of true/false. Example: [[1,2],[2,3,3],[2,3,1],[2,2,2]] → obstacle at 2; a block of 3 does not fit in [0,3]; a block of 1 does; a block of 2 fits in [0,2] → [false, true, true].

Example input[[1,2],[2,3,3],[2,3,1],[2,2,2]]
→
Output[False, True, True]

Why? A number line gets obstacles one at a time. Between them you are asked: can a block of length sz fit somewhere in the part from 0 to x without touching an obstacle?

💡 Key idea Store for each obstacle the gap to the obstacle before it, in a segment tree for 'maximum over a range'. Adding an obstacle splits one gap into two (two point updates). A query takes the maximum gap among obstacles ≤ x, also considering the open stretch from the last obstacle to x.

Edge / failure case a single query, no obstacles → [True]
There are no obstacles at all, so a block of length 5 fits into [0, 5].
